Under these conditions, the probability of leakage to non-computational states per readout is $0.018\pm0.002\%$.

### Paper [319]
Title: Probing chiral topological order of the Kitaev honeycomb on a trapped ion processor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.08939
Authors: Ammar Ali, Joe Gibbs, Keerthi Kumaran, Varadharajan Muruganandam, Bo Xiao, Paul Kairys, G\'abor Hal\'asz, Arnab Banerjee, Phillip C. Lotshaw
Abstract: Kitaev's honeycomb model is a paradigmatic exactly solvable system hosting a quantum spin liquid with non-Abelian anyons and topologically protected edge modes, offering a platform for fault-tolerant quantum computation. However, real candidate Kitaev materials invariably include complex secondary interactions that obscure the realization of spin-liquid behavior and demand novel quantum computational approaches for efficient simulation. Here we report quantum simulations of a 22-site Kitaev honeycomb lattice on a trapped-ion quantum processor, without and with non-integrable Heisenberg interactions that are present in real materials. We develop efficient quantum circuits for ground-state preparation, achieving high accuracy with energy errors equivalent to an effective temperature $\approx 0.2$ (in units of the Kitaev interactions), consistent with the experimentally relevant spin-liquid regime. Starting from these states, we apply controlled perturbations and measure time-dependent spin correlations along the system's edge. In the non-Abelian phase, we observe chiral edge dynamics consistent with a non-zero Chern number, a hallmark of chiral topological order, which vanishes upon transition to the Abelian toric code phase. Extending to the non-integrable Kitaev-Heisenberg model, we find that weak Heisenberg interactions preserve chiral edge dynamics, while stronger couplings suppress them, signaling the breakdown of topological protection. These simulations establish edge-mode dynamical simulations on quantum computers as a staple technique to understand the topological and chiral phases of matter in Hamiltonians beyond integrability.

### Paper [320]
Title: Optimal Calibration of Qubit Detuning and Crosstalk
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10661
Authors: David Shnaiderov, Matan Ben Dov, Yoav Woldiger, Assaf Hamo, Eugene Demler, Emanuele G. Dalla Torre
Abstract: Characterizing and calibrating physical qubits is essential for maintaining the performance of quantum processors. A key challenge in this process is the presence of crosstalk that complicates the estimation of individual qubit detunings. In this work, we derive optimal strategies for estimating detuning and crosstalk parameters by optimizing Ramsey interference experiments using Fisher information and the Cramer-Rao bound. We compare several calibration protocols, including measurements of a single quadrature at multiple times and of two quadratures at a single time, for a fixed number of total measurements. Our results predict that the latter approach yields the highest precision and robustness in both cases of isolated and coupled qubits. We validate experimentally our approach using a single NV center as well as superconducting transmons. Our approach enables accurate parameter extraction with significantly fewer measurements, resulting in up to a 50% reduction in calibration time while maintaining estimation accuracy.

### Paper [321]
Title: Learning to stabilize nonequilibrium phases of matter with active feedback using partial information
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06612
Authors: Giovanni Cemin, Markus Schmitt, Marin Bukov
Abstract: Recent advances in quantum computing experiments have enabled new pathways for feedback control. Here, we investigate the role of information in interactive quantum many-body dynamics to engineer out-of-equilibrium phases of matter. To this end, we train reinforcement learning agents to prevent entanglement spreading in (1+1)-dimensional stabilizer circuits with up to 128 qubits. We find that, above an information threshold, agents learn stochastic near-optimal strategies that prevent the system from reaching volume-law entangled states and instead stabilize area-law scaling. These strategies rely on bottlenecks that divide the system and cap the entanglement in the steady state. Crucially, learned strategies are inherently out of equilibrium, require real-time active feedback, and could not be reproduced by simple human-designed control rules. This work establishes the foundations for information-driven individual control of many interacting quantum degrees of freedom, demonstrating the capabilities of reinforcement learning to stabilize and uncover novel critical properties of many-body nonequilibrium steady states.

### Paper [322]
Title: Ultrafast Optical Probing of Laser Processing
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03220
Authors: J\"orn Bonse
Abstract: Laser treatment has emerged into a cornerstone of industrial manufacturing. This creates the demand to understand in detail the involved physical processes and their dynamics, to control the process, and to keep guard in-situ the laser manufacturing steps. Given the contactless interaction of light with matter, particularly optical methods appear most suitable for probing laser processing. This chapter reviews the current scientific and technological state of analyzing laser-based material processing by short and ultrashort optical pulses. For that, sequential passive interrogation of material properties (via reflection, absorption, scattering, diffraction, interference, or specific nonlinear responses) can be employed through so-called pump-probe techniques (where the time-resolution is not given by the detector response time but by the duration of an electromagnetic probe pulse that is interrogating the transient scenery at a controlled delay (typ. fs to \mu s) with respect to the arrival of the pump laser pulse). These approaches are capable to analyze on microscopic to macroscopic scales a plethora of different processes involved in laser processing, such as heating, melting, ablation, plasma- and shockwave-formation, optical breakdown in surrounding liquids and gases, shielding and accumulation effects, surface solidification and restructuring, etc. A special focus is set on the variety of ultrafast optical imaging schemes for visualizing the dynamics of laser processing at the surface and in the bulk of the irradiated materials, including photography and microscopy, holography, tomography, ptychography, and coherent diffractive imaging. The chapter provides a historic survey, highlights important scientific and technological developments and breakthroughs, discusses selected applications, explores the requirements and current limitations, and sheds light on emerging future trends.

### Paper [323]
Title: How Does Incubation Affect Laser Material Processing?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08326
Authors: Matthias Lenzner, J\"orn Bonse
Abstract: In the last decades, the subject of laser-induced damage (LID) moved from a topic of scientific interest toward laser material processing for technical applications. When using pulsed lasers, the cumulative effect known as incubation is one of the most fundamental features of the processing event. Incubation manifests by the fact that the critical fluence for LID (known as laser-induced damage threshold, LIDT) depends on the number of pulses N exciting one spot on the sample. In most cases, the threshold fluence decreases with N starting from the single-shot ablation threshold and remains constant for large N. No ablation or damage occurs for any N, if the fluence is kept below the multiple-pulse threshold. In contrast, examples where the LIDT increases with N have been reported. The latter effect is known as laser conditioning and is advantageously used when ramping up the power of high-power laser systems. Incubation has been described for many types of solids; the motivation for these comprehensive efforts is twofold. Firstly, one tries to prevent the damage of optical materials in the beam path of high-energy or high-peak-power lasers. Secondly, one deliberately uses this damage for the sculpting of components. In this chapter, we introduce the reader to the mechanisms of incubation. We are going to look at the parameters controlling LID and highlight the peculiarity of the parameter "number of pulses". We will give an overview of the experimental work done in a variety of materials. There are several physical and chemical mechanisms proposed that govern incubation, and there are several mathematical models to describe the behavior of threshold fluence and ablation rate in dependence on the number of pulses. In a few cases, the two classes of mechanisms are even related to each other. Eventually, we will show the implications that incubation has on real-world laser machining.

### Paper [324]
Title: Adversarial Thermodynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08298
Authors: Maite Arcos, Philippe Faist, Takahiro Sagawa, Jonathan Oppenheim
Abstract: In thermodynamics, an agent's ability to extract work is fundamentally constrained by their environment. Traditional frameworks struggle to capture how strategic decision-making under uncertainty, particularly an agent's tolerance for risk, determines the trade-off between extractable work and probability of success in finite-scale experiments. Here, we develop a framework for nonequilibrium thermodynamics based on adversarial resource theories, in which work extraction is modeled as an adversarial game for an agent extracting work. Within this perspective, we consider a Szilard-type engine as a game isomorphic to Kelly gambling, an information-theoretic model of optimal betting under uncertainty -- but with a thermodynamic utility function. Extending the framework to finite-size regimes, we apply a risk-reward trade-off to find an interpretation of the Renyi divergences in terms of extractable work for a given failure probability. By incorporating risk sensitivity via utility functions, we show that the guaranteed amount of work a rational agent would accept instead of undertaking a risky protocol is given by a Renyi divergence. This provides a unified picture of thermodynamics and gambling, and highlights how generalized free energies emerge from an adversarial setup.

### Paper [325]
Title: Hybridized-Mode Parametric Amplifier in Kinetic-Inductance Circuits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03362
Authors: Danial Davoudi, Abdul Mohamed, Shabir Barzanjeh
Abstract: Parametric amplification is essential for quantum measurement, enabling the amplification of weak microwave signals with minimal added noise. While Josephson-junction-based amplifiers have become standard in superconducting quantum circuits, their magnetic sensitivity, limited saturation power, and sub-kelvin operating requirements motivate the development of alternative nonlinear platforms. Here we demonstrate a two-mode kinetic-inductance parametric amplifier based on a pair of capacitively coupled Kerr-nonlinear resonators fabricated from NbTiN and NbN thin films. The distributed Kerr nonlinearity of these materials enables nondegenerate four-wave-mixing amplification with gains approaching 40 dB, gain-bandwidth products up to 6.9 MHz, and 1-dB compression powers two to three orders of magnitude higher than those of state-of-the-art Josephson amplifiers. A coupled-mode theoretical model accurately captures the pump-induced modification of the hybridized modes and quantitatively reproduces the observed signal and idler responses. The NbN device exhibits a significantly larger Kerr coefficient and superior gain-bandwidth performance, highlighting the advantages of high-kinetic-inductance materials. Our results establish coupled kinetic-inductance resonators as a robust platform for broadband, high-power, and magnetically resilient quantum-limited amplification, offering a scalable route for advanced readout in superconducting qubits, spin ensembles, quantum dots, and other microwave-quantum technologies.

### Paper [326]
Title: Electric birefringence in Euler-Heisenberg pseudo-electrodynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21143
Authors: M. J. Neves
Abstract: The fermion sector of the pseudo-quantum electrodynamics is integrated functionally to generate a non-linear electrodynamics, that it is called Euler-Heisenberg pseudo-electrodynamics. A non-local Chern-Simons topological term is added to the original lagrangian of the pseudo-quantum electrodynamics in which a most complete electrodynamics gauge invariant in 1+2 dimensions is proposed. As consequence of the fermionic sector, we obtain a non-linear contribution in the electromagnetic fields that breaks the Lorentz symmetry due to Fermi velocity. From the Euler-Heisenberg pseudo-electrodynamics, we study the properties of the plane wave propagating in a planar medium under an uniform and constant electromagnetic background field. The properties of the planar material are discussed through the electric permittivity tensor and magnetic permeability, that are functions of the frequency, wavelength and of the background fields. The dispersion relations and the refractive index are calculated in the presence of a uniform magnetic field, and also in the case only of an electric background field. The birefringence phenomenon emerges only when the electric background field is considered.

### Paper [327]
Title: Collaboration drives phase transitions towards cooperation in prisoner's dilemma
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11601
Authors: Joy Das Bairagya, Jonathan Newton, Sagar Chakraborty
Abstract: The emergence of cooperation among self-interested individuals is a central puzzle across biology and the social sciences. While evolutionary game theory has identified mechanisms such as spatial structure and group selection to sustain cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma, most models assume individuals revise their strategies independently. In reality, social organisms frequently form temporary coalitions to coordinate their decisions. We introduce a \emph{collaboration ring model} to investigate how such joint strategy updating affects the prisoner's dilemma. Individuals update strategies either alone---choosing a best response---or by forming coalitions where actions change only if every member is at least as well off and at least one is strictly better off. Despite individual incentives favouring defection, we show that cooperation emerges through non-equilibrium phase transitions governed by the propensity to collaborate and the benefit of cooperation. Below a critical threshold, defection dominates; above it, cooperation persists through a continuous or a discontinuous transition. Using pair approximation, we derive phase boundaries that align closely with stochastic simulations. Finally, we demonstrate that these results are robust to variations in neighbourhood and coalition size, suggesting that collaborative decision-making is a general mechanism for sustaining cooperation. Our work has potential connections to both the mutualism phenomenon in non-humans and the shared intentionality phenomenon in humans.

### Paper [328]
Title: A Reduced Order Model for First-Principles Atomistic Simulations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22390
Authors: Siu Wun Cheung, Youngsoo Choi, Jonas Kaufman, Daniel Osei-Kuffuor, Jean-Luc Fattebert
Abstract: To leverage the redundancy between the electronic structure computed for various model parameters such as varying atomic positions, we present a data-driven modeling framework for Kohn-Sham Density Functional Theory that bypasses the explicit optimization of electronic wavefunctions. We sample \textit{a priori} representative atomic configurations and construct a low-dimensional basis that efficiently approximates the electronic structure subspace. Subsequently, we employ this reduced basis in an iterative solver for the electronic single particle density matrix, thereby enabling the efficient determination of ground state without iterative wavefunction optimization. We demonstrate the accuracy of our approach in a Born-Oppenheimer molecular dynamics of a water molecule, showing that the resulting simulations accurately reproduce key structural properties, such as bond lengths and bond angle, obtained from full first-principles molecular dynamics. This work highlights the potential of data-driven approaches to develop low-dimensional electronic structure representations for first-principles simulations.

### Paper [329]
Title: From Accurate Quantum Chemistry to Converged Thermodynamics for Ion Pairing in Solution
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06800
Authors: Niamh O'Neill, Benjamin X. Shi, William C. Witt, Blake I. Armstrong, Asmita Niyogi, William J. Baldwin, Paolo Raiteri, Christoph Schran, Angelos Michaelides, Julian D. Gale
Abstract: Quantitative prediction of thermodynamic properties in solution is essential for translating atomistic simulations into reliable chemical insight. As an exemplar system, the behaviour of CaCO$_3$ in water has been widely studied to understand its mineralization in seawater, with potential implications for carbon-capture strategies. However, making accurate computational predictions has been a long-standing challenge, requiring both highly accurate electronic structure methods and extensive statistical sampling. Here, we combine advances in machine learning and electronic structure theory to fully resolve the ion pairing free energy of CaCO$_3$ with explicit solvation. We show that achieving quantitative agreement with experiment requires going beyond density functional theory up to the ''gold-standard'' coupled cluster theory with single, double, and perturbative triple excitations [CCSD(T)]. We generate a set of systematically improvable models, enabling reliable insights into the initial association mechanism of Ca and CO$_3$ ions prior to nucleation while fully quantifying enthalpic and entropic effects. These quantitatively accurate models further resolve a long-standing lack of consensus surrounding the hydration mechanism of the calcium ion in solution. Our results demonstrate that CCSD(T)-level thermodynamic predictions of complex aqueous systems can now be routinely achieved.

### Paper [330]
Title: Employment-access constraints in the 15-minute city
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12122
Authors: Marc Barthelemy
Abstract: The `15-minute city' has emerged as an influential urban-planning paradigm, promoting proximity to work and essential services. Its feasibility for journey-to-work commuting, however, has rarely been examined quantitatively. Here, we show that employment proximity is constrained by heterogeneity in the number of employees per firm. Combining urban geometry with empirically observed firm-size distributions, we derive a lower bound on commuting time that holds even under optimal workplace placement. This bound reveals a sharp transition: when employment is sufficiently unevenly distributed across firms, no spatial rearrangement of workplaces can provide uniformly short commutes. Applied to Paris and its near suburbs, with area $A=762~\mathrm{km}^2$ and empirical firm-size exponent $\hat{\gamma}\simeq 1.38$, the model predicts that only about $37\%$ of workers can reach their workplace within $15$ minutes by walking. Cycling increases this share to about $67\%$, but still leaves roughly one third of the workforce beyond the 15-minute threshold. Achieving universal short commutes would therefore require economic restructuring, differentiated mobility strategies, remote work, or a longer target time. Our results suggest replacing the universal 15-minute target, for commuting, by a city-specific benchmark determined by firm-size heterogeneity and urban scale.

### Paper [331]
Title: Thermalization Across Scales on a Digital Quantum Processor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12236
Authors: Faisal Alam, Marcos Crichigno, Elizabeth Crosson, Steven T. Flammia, Filippo Maria Gambetta, Max Hunter Gordon, Michael Kreshchuk, Ashley Montanaro, Alberto Nocera, Raul A. Santos
Abstract: Thermalization of isolated quantum many-body systems normally unfolds via dynamical spreading of entanglement across progressively increasing spatial scales. The speed of this dynamical process, however, can be affected by the presence of local disorder. Here we study the crossover between slow and fast thermalization in a disordered two-dimensional Heisenberg Floquet model using digital quantum simulations with up to 100 superconducting qubits. Our experiment measures subsystem purities and $Z$-basis marginal collision entropies for patches of up to $3\times 3$ qubits. We resolve the onset of thermalization across scales by tracking each quantity's approach to its asymptotic value. In regimes where the system is moderately entangled, we validate our calibrated quantum simulations against state-of-the-art classical simulations. Near the crossover to fast thermalization, the classical methods no longer reliably converge due to volume-law growth of entanglement. By exploring this computationally hard regime, our work establishes a frontier of applications for digital quantum computers in probing complex many-body thermalization.By exploring this computationally hard regime, our work establishes a frontier of applications for digital quantum computers in probing complex many-body thermalization.

### Paper [332]
Title: Experimental realization of a $\cos(2\varphi)$ transmon qubit
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13114
Authors: Erwan Roverc'h, Alvise Borgognoni, Marius Villiers, Kyrylo Gerashchenko, W. Clarke Smith, Christopher Wilson, Benoit Dou\c{c}ot, Alexandru Petrescu, Philippe Campagne-Ibarcq, Zaki Leghtas
Abstract: Symmetry-based selection rules make some atomic states remarkably long-lived, a protection superconducting circuits usually lack. We report coherent control and single-shot readout of a 13.6 MHz $\cos 2\varphi$ qubit in the soft-transmon regime. It consists of an island shunted to ground through an interferometer of Josephson junctions and superinductances that favors tunneling of pairs of Cooper pairs, so that Cooper-pair parity symmetry protects the qubit from dielectric loss. We measure a qubit charge matrix element over 100 times smaller than that of the circuit's unprotected plasmon transition, placing the dielectric limit on the qubit lifetime above 10 ms. The measured $T_1 = 70\,\mu$s is instead predicted, with no free parameters, by the $1/f$ flux noise in the interferometer loop that limits dephasing ($T_{2\mathrm{E}} = 2.5\,\mu$s). This experiment demonstrates that Cooper-pair parity symmetry protects against the dielectric loss it targets, and that the remaining limit is the interferometer's sensitivity to flux noise, which larger superinductances are predicted to reduce without compromising protection.

### Paper [333]
Title: Decoherence and the Reemergence of Coherence From a Superconducting "Horizon"
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16765
Authors: Eric J. Sung, Charles A. Stafford
Abstract: In a recent paper [arXiv:2205.06279], Danielson et al. demonstrated that the mere presence of a black hole causes universal decoherence of quantum superpositions (dubbed the DSW decoherence). We analyze decoherence in a superconducting analogue [arXiv:1709.06154] of the event horizon of a late-stage black hole, where Andreev reflection plays the role of Hawking radiation. We consider a normal metal interferometer threaded by an Aharonov-Bohm flux, where one of the arms of the interferometer is coupled to a superconductor by a tunnel coupling of varying strength. At absolute zero temperature and for weak coupling, we find that the scattering states of the interferometer are decohered by Andreev reflection, a nontrivial manifestation of the proximity effect analogous to DSW decoherence from the event horizon of a black hole. However, for increasing coupling strength to the superconductor, we find a reemergence of coherence via resonant tunneling through Andreev bound states. This suggests the existence of an analogue gravitational phenomenon wherein transmission mediated by virtual Hawking radiation leads to a reemergence of coherence in an interferometer placed near a black hole's event horizon.

### Paper [334]
Title: Tuning of quantum nanoscaled friction within the Prandtl-Tomlinson model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27216
Authors: Dai-Nam Le, Lilia M. Woods
Abstract: Nanoscaled is a fundamental tribological phenomenon with complex behavior of its dynamical force. Here, we utilize the Prandtl-Tomlinson framework to investigate systematically the different means of control of the frictional force at the quantum and classical levels. It is found that the frictional dynamics can be controlled by the corrugation and characteristic length ratio parameters dependent upon properties of the nanoparticle-chain system. In addition to the stick-slip regime, other types of motion are uncovered, highlighting the richness of the frictional dynamics. The importance of Landau-Zener tunneling for the quantum motion is also analyzed. These findings provide valuable insights for interpreting experimental observations and controlling quantum frictional behavior in nanoscale systems.

### Paper [335]
Title: Weak ergodicity breaking captured from short-time dynamcis and graph theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00094
Authors: Heiko Georg Menzler, Rafa{\l} \'Swi\k{e}tek, Mari Carmen Ba\~nuls, Fabian Heidrich-Meisner
Abstract: We study the onset of weak ergodicity violations in closed quantum many-body systems and focus on cases in which they occur through a transition that is controlled by a model parameter, a scenario called a weak ergodicity-breaking transition. This situation is realized in the Rosenzweig-Porter model and the quantum sun model, which we investigate here. Our analysis is based on representing quantum systems in Fock space and examining the short-time dynamics of density autocorrelation functions. From these, we extract proxies for decay rates for each individual basis state. As a first main result, we show that the distribution of such decay rates captures known weak ergodicity-breaking transitions in both models. As a second main result, we establish a connection between the decay rates of density autocorrelations and graph-theoretical measures. Concretely, we demonstrate that moments of the distribution of the recently introduced graph-energy centrality also detect the weak ergodicity transitions, which is inherited from the behavior of the decay rates. While most numerical tools are limited to small system sizes, the quantities studied in our work can be calculated analytically for large systems of many hundreds of sites and in some cases, even in the thermodynamic limit.

### Paper [336]
Title: The Interplay of Data Structure and Imbalance in the Learning Dynamics of Diffusion Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06367
Authors: Flavio Nicoletti, Chenxiao Ma, Enrico Ventura, Luca Saglietti, Stefano Sarao Mannelli
Abstract: Real-world datasets are inherently heterogeneous, yet how per-class structural differences and sampling imbalance shape the training dynamics of diffusion models---and potentially exacerbate disparities---remains poorly understood. While models typically transition from an initial phase of generalization to memorizing the training set, existing theory assumes homogeneous data, leaving open how class imbalance and heterogeneity reshape these dynamics. In this work, we develop a high-dimensional analytical framework to study class-dependent learning in score-based diffusion models. Analyzing a random-features model trained on Gaussian mixtures, we derive the feature-covariance spectrum to characterize per-class generalization and memorization times. We reveal the explicit hierarchy governing these dynamics: class variance is the primary determinant of learning order---consistently favoring higher-variance classes---while centroid geometry and sampling imbalance play secondary roles. Specifically, sampling imbalance acts as a modulator that can reverse this ordering and, under strong imbalance, forces minority classes to acquire distinct, delayed inference times during backward diffusion. Together, these results suggest that diffusion models can memorize some classes while others remain insufficiently learned. We validate our theoretical predictions empirically using U-Net models trained on Fashion MNIST.

### Paper [337]
Title: A reason why we do not observe Schr\"odinger's cats
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16148
Authors: Fabio Siringo
Abstract: A reason is discussed (may be not the only one) for why we do not see any superposition of macroscopic states in the real world. Under the general assumption that quantum macrostates are statistical ensembles of microstates, it is shown that any superposition of macrostates is reduced in a very short time by the unitary dynamics of the ordinary Schr\"odinger equation, deducing the Born rule without having to postulate it. In more detail, the macroscopic and microscopic degrees of freedom are decoupled in the Schr\"odinger equation, yielding an effective stochastic equation for the macroscopic variables, with the ensemble average of the microscopic amplitudes that acts as a self-generated internal white noise. The stochastic equation is shown to be a reducing It\^o equation if some general causality conditions are met, predicting a very quick collapse of any macroscopic superposition upon formation, with probabilities which satisfy the Born rule. In the context of the von Neumann measurement scheme, the relevance of the result is discussed as a simple dynamical solution of the measurement problem.

### Paper [338]
Title: Identifiability and endpoint reconstruction of time-dependent Jacobi Hamiltonians from projective trajectory jets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22796
Authors: A. M. Tishin
Abstract: We study the identifiability of smooth time-dependent Jacobi Hamiltonians from phase-resolved Schrodinger trajectories. When the initial state is localized at a chain endpoint, we prove that the projective trajectory jet through order M+1 uniquely determines the first M couplings, their logarithmic time derivatives, and the associated diagonal energy differences at preparation. This derivative order is necessary in the unrestricted real-Jacobi class. If only the first K projective coordinates are observed, even their full Taylor jets leave every deeper instantaneous layer undetermined. We also construct evolutions that are indistinguishable on an observation window separated from preparation but have different endpoint coupling scales and rates. We identify two ways to restore local identifiability: first-order projective and reference-population data at generic times, or second-order data from ceil(M/2) suitable site preparations for independent coupling rates. Statistical distinguishability bounds and finite-window simulations quantify the effects of noise, nuisance coefficients, and acquisition design.

### Paper [339]
Title: Ulam Approximation for Nonautonomous Systems: Equivariant Measures and Linear Response
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02889
Authors: Stefano Galatolo, Valerio Lucarini, Isaia Nisoli
Abstract: Despite the prevalence of nonautonomous systems in applications, their statistical properties are much less understood than in the autonomous setting. Building on recent results on response theory for nonautonomous systems, we study the approximation of equivariant families and of their linear response by Ulam-type finite-dimensional reductions. First, we show that coarse-graining procedures associated with the classical Ulam method, and more generally with suitable finite-element projections, such as those often associated with the Koopman/Kolmogorov operator-informed analysis of dynamical systems, provide rigorous approximation of equivariant families for sequential systems with memory loss. Second, for systems whose transfer operators are regularising, we prove that the linear response of the reduced finite-state Markov model converges to the projected linear response of the original system. We illustrate the results with numerical experiments on simple but representative time-dependent diffusive models. These results provide a rigorous foundation for the use of Markov approximations in the study of statistical properties of nonautonomous complex systems which almost invariably relies on finite-scale and finite-precision descriptions of their states and dynamics.

### Paper [340]
Title: An Asymptotic Theory of Chain-of-Thought in In-Context Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03217
Authors: Kaito Takanami, Cengiz Pehlevan
Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has become a widely used mechanism for eliciting multi-step reasoning in large language models by generating intermediate reasoning steps at inference time. Yet the scaling behavior of generalization with CoT depth remains poorly understood. To address this question, we study a theoretically solvable model of CoT for in-context weight prediction in linear regression, where test-time reasoning is represented as an iterative refinement of the weight-parameter estimate. Using tools from random matrix theory under high-dimensional asymptotics, we derive an exact formula for the generalization error as a function of reasoning depth, pretraining data amount, and context length. Our analysis reveals a sharp phase transition separating exponential and polynomial improvement, saturation, and overthinking, and characterizes how the optimal reasoning depth scales. We further show that deeper reasoning is most effective with sufficiently rich pretraining and in-context information, whereas limited pretraining or context makes longer reasoning prone to error amplification or saturation. We also validate these predictions through experiments on fully learned linear attention and softmax attention models. Our results provide a unified theoretical account of how test-time CoT depth affects generalization.

### Paper [341]
Title: Subspace-selective unitary manipulation based on the Hilbert-space symmetric structures in the multiple-quantum operator algebra spaces in the quantum-computing speedup theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03859
Authors: Xijia Miao
Abstract: The quantum-computing speedup theory considers the symmetric structures and properties of quantum systems as the fundamental Quantum-Computing-Speedup (QCS) resources which are responsible for exponentially speeding up quantum computing and simulating. At present a large and important problem is how to make use of the fundamental QCS resources to speed up essentially quantum computing and simulating. Here the author makes a great effort toward solving this important problem. The theoretical research work in this paper is mainly divided into the two Parts I and II. The Part I investigates mainly the multiple-quantum operator algebra spaces. And the relationships are analyzed among the multiple-quantum operator algebra spaces, quantum simulating for the unitary time-evolutional processes, and the fundamental QCS resources which exist in the different kinds of basic quantum spaces: the multiple-quantum operator algebra spaces, the density operator spaces, and the Hilbert spaces. It concludes that the multiple-quantum operator algebra space must be positioned as the central place where the fundamental QCS resources are exploited to speed up essentially quantum computing and simulating. The Part II investigates mainly the subspace-selective unitary manipulation based on the Hilbert-space symmetric structures. Recognize that the multiple-quantum operator algebra space is the central place. Then those fundamental QCS resources original from the Hilbert space (a quantum-state space) must be explicitly taken into account in the multiple-quantum operator algebra space (a linear operator space). This is an important problem. The subspace-selective unitary manipulation is able to solve this problem. It aims to harness the fundamental QCS resources original from the Hilbert space to speed up quantum computing and simulating in the multiple-quantum operator algebra space.

### Paper [342]
Title: Retarded Correlators of Charge Transport in a Magnetic Field
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08139
Authors: Xuan Zhao, Qiuze Sun, Yi Wang, Jin Hu
Abstract: We study charge transport in a magnetized relativistic plasma using kinetic theory within the relaxation-time approximation. By exactly solving the linearized Boltzmann equation in a uniform magnetic field, we obtain an analytic solution for the distribution function in terms of Bessel functions. Using this solution, we compute the full set of retarded current-current correlators and verify the Ward identities. In the hydrodynamic limit, we extract the charge diffusion modes, demonstrating that the transverse diffusion coefficient is strongly suppressed by the magnetic field, scaling as $1/B_0^2$ in the strong-field regime, while the longitudinal diffusion remains unaffected. Furthermore, we analyze the non-hydrodynamic branch cuts in the complex frequency plane, determining their kinematic thresholds and identifying the underlying wave-particle interactions as longitudinal Landau damping and transverse cyclotron damping.

### Paper [343]
Title: Hydrogen-Bond Donor-Acceptor Imbalance in Low-Frequency Terahertz Water Spectra
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06780
Authors: Lilian Najm Alsayed, Florian Pabst, Giuseppe Cassone, Ali Hassanali, Fabio Novelli
Abstract: The low-frequency dielectric response of liquid water is commonly described by a dominant Debye relaxation together with additional faster contributions whose microscopic origin remains debated. Here we show that the dielectric function of water between 0.14 and 1.21 THz can be represented by a collective Debye relaxation plus a Drude-Smith term constrained to the zero-dc-conductivity limit. The Drude-Smith spectral weight increases upon heating pure H2O from 20 C to 50 C and decreases upon isotopic substitution (D2O at 20 C vs. H2O at 20 C). Molecular dynamics simulations including nuclear quantum effects show correlated changes in the population of water molecules with unequal numbers of donated and accepted hydrogen-bonds. Ab-initio-based spectra calculations further indicate that the ~0.1-1 THz response contains both nuclear-motion and explicit electronic-polarisation/charge-redistribution contributions. We therefore interpret the excess low-frequency THz response as a localised, mixed nuclear-electronic dielectric response correlated with transient donor-acceptor imbalance in the hydrogen-bond network.

### Paper [344]
Title: High-purity entanglement mediated by magnons despite weak coupling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19533
Authors: Sanchar Sharma
Abstract: Entangling distant spins via a shared magnonic bus typically faces a tradeoff: stronger spin-magnon coupling increases the entanglement fidelity, but also the spin decay rate. We propose a protocol that breaks this tradeoff. The magnons couple only to a transition outside the computational basis, in which the Bell state is created. Our protocol is probabilistic, and weak coupling reduces the success probability but not the fidelity. We analyze a setup where the spins are two nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers near a magnetic wire. In the absence of NV dephasing, the protocol reaches unit fidelity with a maximally entangled state, for arbitrarily weak coupling. In our simulations via the Monte-Carlo wavefunction approach, the NV-magnon coupling is taken to be one-third of the magnon linewidth. Considering finite NV dephasing, we predict a fidelity of >0.99 for a state-of-the-art rate, and an optimal fidelity of 0.91 for a moderate rate, both at 0.6% success probability.

### Paper [345]
Title: Determining Critical Temperature Differences of Low-Temperature-Differential Stirling Engines: Nonlinear Dynamics Approach
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26539
Authors: Momoko Amemiya, Yuki Izumida
Abstract: While the low-temperature-differential (LTD) Stirling engines are innovative engines that can operate with low temperature differentials in our daily life, the problem of determining the critical temperature differences below which the engine ceases to rotate remains unexplored. In this study, we solve this problem using a nonlinear dynamics approach. We derive the self-consistent equations that determine the critical temperature differences as homoclinic bifurcation points of a dynamical model of the LTD Stirling engines. The solutions of the self-consistent equations reveal a combination of parameters that determines the critical temperature differences. This enables us to establish the fundamental design principles for improving the performance of the LTD Stirling engines beyond empirical designs.

### Paper [346]
Title: Two-photon interference from as-grown InAsP/InP quantum dots under detuned excitation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26957
Authors: Maja Wasiluk, Anna Musia{\l}, Pawe{\l} Mrowi\'nski, Johann Peter Reithmaier, Mohamed Benyoucef, Wojciech Rudno-Rudzi\'nski
Abstract: In this study, we investigate as-grown InAsP/InP quantum dots emitting in the third telecommunication window. The investigated quantum dots are embedded directly in an aluminium-free InP matrix without additional barrier material. A large excitation-emission detuning of 32 meV enables efficient suppression of scattered laser light while retaining several advantages of near-resonant excitation. The single-photon nature of the emission is confirmed by a Hanbury Brown and Twiss experiment, yielding a raw second-order autocorrelation value of $g_{\mathrm{raw}}^{(2)}(0)=0.076(6)$. A Hong-Ou-Mandel measurement is used to determine the degree of indistinguishability of single photons and reveals as-measured visibilities of $V=0.038(8)$ and $V=0.108(18)$ for excitation pulse separations of 13.1 ns and 5.3 ns, respectively. We observe finite photon indistinguishability in as-grown InAsP/InP quantum dots under detuned quasi-resonant excitation, without deliberate Purcell enhancement.

### Paper [347]
Title: Quantum fluctuation relations in first-detection processes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06194
Authors: A. Imparato
Abstract: We derive three quantum fluctuation relations for systems undergoing repeated projective measurements. These fluctuation relations characterize the work that can be extracted from a quantum device when a first-detection event triggers a mechanical operation leading to positive work production. The correction to the standard quantum Jarzynski equality depends logarithmically on the mean first-detection time for the time-reversed dynamics. Application of Jensen's inequality leads to fundamental limits on both the total work involved in the repeated measurements and final mechanical operation, and the extracted work alone. An analogous inequality is derived for the work performed exclusively during the sequence of repeated projective measurements. The general case of a device connected to an external environment is also considered.

### Paper [348]
Title: Dissipative framework for subsystem dynamics of noninteracting quantum chains
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21336
Authors: Michele Coppola, Mari Carmen Ba\~nuls, Zala Lenar\v{c}i\v{c}
Abstract: When only local observables of a many-body quantum system are of interest, it is desirable to formulate a reduced description within the Hilbert space of the corresponding subsystem, with the remaining degrees of freedom traced out and acting as an environment. Assuming initially uncorrelated states and Gaussian environments, we develop a framework for reconstructing the local dynamical generator of noninteracting quantum chains, with polynomial computational effort. As an application, we consider two representative models: a bipartitioned Kitaev chain and a Kitaev chain boundary-coupled to a fully connected free-fermion environment. In both models, strong subsystem-environment coupling leads to non-Markovian dynamics characterized by ballistic spreading of the Lindblad dissipator support within the subsystem. On the other hand, weak coupling to a fully connected environment yields predominantly boundary-localized, Markovian dissipation. Our work highlights the implications of subsystem-environment correlations on the generator of local dynamics, beyond the conventional weak-coupling approximations.

### Paper [349]
Title: Collective photon echoes in the Tavis-Cummings model: distribution independence, two detuning regimes, and the Dicke ladder
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21442
Authors: Michael Tavis
Abstract: An ensemble of $N$ two-level molecules prepared in its ground state and sharing a lossless cavity with a weak field does not simply absorb: the intensity collapses and then recurs, in a train of collective photon echoes. Working from the exact solution of the Tavis-Cummings model in the few-photon regime $\bar n<N$, we confirm the echo time $\tau_E=4\pi\sqrt{N+\Delta}/g$ at resonance from $N=5$ to $N=400$, and separate it from the alternative $4\pi\sqrt{N-\bar n+\Delta}/g$ by a direct test: the recurrence period is independent of $\bar n$ to $0.8\%$ across a factor of six in $\bar n$, where the alternative predicts shifts of up to $17\%$. The initial state, the ground state, requires no preparation. The echo time is independent of the initial photon distribution - coherent, thermal, squeezed and oscillatory-squeezed distributions with variances from 2 to 34 all recur together - but the echo amplitude is not: it varies at the tens-of-percent level at fixed mean, including a factor of 1.8 with the squeezing phase. Detuning organizes the dynamics into two clean regimes separated by a fragmented crossover; the echo time in the dispersive branch is exactly one-half that of the resonant branch, a factor derived from second-order dispersive perturbation theory with leading correction $5Ng^2/\delta^2$, and sufficient detuning removes the dependence on the initial Dicke state. For arbitrary initial $|r=N/2,m\rangle$, emission replaces absorption at $m\simeq-N/2+\bar n$, and the echo envelope acquires one component per step up the ladder. Lindblad simulations with all loss channels acting simultaneously, and full-Hilbert-space disorder simulations, show the first echoes to be observable at $N\sim5$-$20$ on an existing five-qubit superconducting device: the echo survives cavity decay with contrast $\sim e^{-\kappa\tau_E/2}$, photons being shielded while resident in the emitters.

### Paper [350]
Title: Multiphysics simulation of all-optical switching and sequential magneto-optic readout in an integrated Si$_3$N$_4$/GdFeCo waveguide
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22969
Authors: Muhammad Arief Mulyana, Budi Adiperdana, Ayi Bahtiar
Abstract: Femtosecond pulses guided in a silicon nitride waveguide have recently switched a Co/Gd magnetic element patterned on top of it, a step towards magnetic memory built into photonic circuits. The guided mode heats such an element non-uniformly, and, to our knowledge, no calculation has yet followed the written state from this heating to an optical readout. Here we model both steps for an 8 nm GdFeCo film inserted across a Si$_3$N$_4$ waveguide. A three-dimensional finite-difference time-domain calculation gives the absorbed pump energy. This energy drives a four-temperature model and calibrated stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Bloch dynamics, and a later probe pulse reads the recovered state. At the reference fluence, a single pump pulse reverses 86% of the film from either saturated state, and this fraction changes little over a 2.4-fold range of incident fluence. The unswitched cells lie where the absorbed fluence stays below the single-cell threshold, and a threshold rule applied to the absorption map reproduces the full calculation. When the film starts from zero magnetization, thermal noise sets the direction of each recovered local moment at random. Between the saturated and random states, the probe energy fractions change by at most 0.02. The magnetic Faraday and Kerr rotations are far more sensitive and become about 12 and 130 times smaller, respectively, in the random state than in a saturated film. The reflected signal also contains a magnetization-independent geometric background, which can be removed by subtracting a reference signal. The model therefore predicts that a guided probe can distinguish the written states through its polarization. It assumes a homogeneous film and omits heat flow and exchange between neighbouring cells.

### Paper [351]
Title: Universal Defect Statistics in Reverse Quenches
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14960
Authors: Eduard J. Braun, Daniel Rubin, Margaux Cartier, Gerhard Z\"urn, Matthias Weidem\"uller
Abstract: We extend the generalized Kibble-Zurek mechanism (gKZM) to reverse quench protocols using a stochastic description of defect formation finding that the mean defect density after a reverse quench equals twice the forward quench defect-number variance per unit volume. This relation, universally valid within the stochastic framework of the gKZM, makes the forward quench variance accessible through a reverse quench defect density measurement. We verify the equality analytically and numerically in the uniform one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, and numerically in its bond-disordered variant, including a regime where defect scaling departs from a pure power-law in quench time. Our result also suggests an experimental route to probing quantum critical behavior: reverse quenches that begin and end in the paramagnetic phase may allow to reveal critical exponents using global magnetic-field control and magnetization measurements, without resolving individual defects.

### Paper [352]
Title: Upper deviations of the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick free energy: the fifth-order law and the onset of constrained Parisi measures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06480
Authors: Yan Ru Pei
Abstract: Let $Z_N$ be the partition function of the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model at inverse temperature $\beta>1$. Replica theory (Kondor near the critical temperature, Parisi and Rizzo at all temperatures) predicts that the probability that $N^{-1}\log Z_N$ exceeds its typical value by $\delta$ decays like $\exp(-Na\delta^{6/5})$ with an explicit $a$; Aronow and Lopatto recently proved bounds of this order. The exponent is the Legendre transform of the Parisi problem constrained to carry an atom $\theta$ at zero. We show that the constrained minimum exceeds the equilibrium free energy by $c_\beta\theta^5(1+o(1))$, where $c_\beta=\frac{9}{640}\beta^3\rho_\beta(0)^{-3}$ and $\rho_\beta(0)>0$ is the density at zero of the Parisi measure. With the fractional-moment formula this gives the exponent $\frac56(6c_\beta)^{-1/5}\delta^{6/5}(1+o(1))$ as $N\to\infty$ and then $\delta\to0$. The constrained minimizer has a marginal first positive contact, to leading order at $3\theta/(2\rho_\beta(0))$ with an atom $\theta/2$. This plateau calculus applies to every mixture with $\xi'''(0)=0$ whose Parisi measure accumulates at zero; a small field $h$ opens a gap of order $|h|^{2/3}$, and an atom of the Parisi measure at zero, present in every pure $p$-spin model with $p\ge3$, makes the rate leave zero linearly. Near criticality the constant tends to Kondor's value $9/5120$ and the marginal regime ends at a de Almeida-Thouless threshold. For the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model at every $\beta>1$, a finite-step curvature inequality from an unrefereed preprint shows that every constrained minimizer either has an interval of positive contacts, all marginal, or has exactly two atoms, and that the marginal regime ends at a unique de Almeida-Thouless threshold $\theta_c(\beta)$. The laws at every $\beta>1$ use that the Parisi measure accumulates at zero, a theorem of Lopatto also proved in earlier work of the author.

### Paper [353]
Title: TheoPhysEval: Assessing AI agents at autonomous theoretical physics problem solving
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16733
Authors: A. Ramesh Chandra, Aaghaz Mahajan, Ainesh Sanyal, Mianqi Wang, Shubhadeep Chakraborty, Sudip Chakraborty, Shailesh Chandrasekharan, Rama Govindarajan, Akshit Goyal, Jonathan J. Heckman, Yasir Iqbal, Divya Jaganathan, Saumav Kapoor, Andreas Karch, Pushkar Khandare, Sandeep Krishna, Anupam Kundu, Alok Laddha, Tongyan Lin, Swarnendu Maity, Nilmani Mathur, Shani Nadir Meynet, Alessandro Mininno, Abhijeet Mishra, Vidur Niranjan, Abhishodh Prakash, Pratik Rath, Durbar Roy, Debargha Sarkar, Srikanth Sastry, Gary Shiu, Aninda Sinha, Thomas Van Riet, Praneeth Netrapalli, Suvrat Raju
Abstract: We present a suite of 50 unpublished research-level theoretical physics problems with verifiable answers. Without AI assistance, a typical problem in our collection could be solved by a world expert in about a day or less but would take considerably longer for experts in adjacent specialties. We test the ability of off-the-shelf AI agents to tackle these problems autonomously, without human hints. When given access to tools available in a realistic setting, including the ability to search the literature and write scripts for numerical and symbolic work, the best-performing agent, GPT-6-Astra harnessed in Codex, obtains the final answers with a near-perfect accuracy of 98% at an average cost under USD 3 per problem. On average, agents show only small changes in accuracy when denied web search and access to background research papers, suggesting significant memorization of the literature. These results suggest that state-of-the-art agents can accurately perform concrete calculations required across theoretical physics research. We comment on the broader implications of these findings and discuss possible explanations for the current lack of agent-led breakthroughs in theoretical physics despite their outstanding problem-solving abilities.

### Paper [354]
Title: Monodromy Defects of 3D Wilson-Fisher Criticality on the Fuzzy Sphere
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31269
Authors: Yin Tang, W. Zhu
Abstract: Monodromy line defects arise as boundaries of topological symmetry surfaces, providing a natural route to conformal defects with implications in condensed matter, gauge theories and quantum information. However, their interacting dynamics remains difficult to characterize non-perturbatively. Here we realize monodromy boundary conditions in the fuzzy-sphere regularization by threading a symmetry-selective Aharonov-Bohm flux through the two poles of the sphere. We apply this construction to the $Z_2$ twist of the 3D Ising conformal field theory (CFT), the $\pi$-twist of the 3D O(2) CFT, and three inequivalent monodromies of O(3) (centre twist, z-axis $\pi$-twist and reflection). For each defect we extract the low-lying defect spectrum together with bulk-to-defect and defect-defect operator product expansion coefficients, which characterize the operators living on the defect line, their mutual interactions, and how they couple to the critical bulk. Among the resolved low-lying defect primaries we find no relevant scalar perturbation preserving all symmetries of the corresponding defect; the relevant deformations identified break at least one preserved symmetry, providing evidence that these monodromy defects are stable against symmetry-preserving relevant perturbations. For the partial twists of O(3), we derive the flow of the symmetry-preserving untwisted zero-mode mass generated by the bulk interaction. Its weak-coupling runaway near four dimensions motivates a careful distinction between the microscopic absence of pinning, the free reference background, and a possible infrared defect fixed point in three dimensions. These results establish a systematic set of non-perturbative conformal data for monodromy defects beyond the Ising twist, turning the symmetry flux into a tunable knob of the fuzzy-sphere regularization for exploring the response of critical matter to distinct defects.

### Paper [355]
Title: Onsager Algebras and Lattice Fermions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31653
Authors: Ioanna Kourkoulou, David Tong
Abstract: We study the exact Onsager algebra of free lattice fermions in staggered, Wilson, and overlap incarnations, working in both Hamiltonian and Euclidean formulations in four spacetime dimensions. In each case, we show that the exact lattice algebra emanates the full chiral symmetry of the low-energy theory: if there are $N$ massless Dirac fermions in the continuum, the Onsager algebra reduces to a $U(2N)$ chiral symmetry. The details of which generators are quantised or ultra-local differ between formulations. There is also a trade-off between the locality of generators and the maximum compact subgroup of the Onsager algebra.

### Paper [356]
Title: SHAPE: a sparse Hamiltonian approach for prescribing eigenstates in metamaterials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37248
Authors: Yutao Chen, Tao Xiao, Jing Li, Aodong Li, Biye Xie
Abstract: Metamaterials, which store, guide and emit waves through their eigenstates, have enabled the development of devices with diverse functionalities. Yet the spatial profiles of conventional metamaterials' eigenstates are typically determined by their pre-determined structures (Hamiltonians). Here we develop SHAPE, a sparse Hamiltonian approach that enables the realization of prescribed, spectrally isolated eigenstates in physically realizable metamaterials. The resulting Hamiltonian geometries obey a boundary law, with the required nonlocality determined solely by single-contact boundary sites ($|E_{\mathrm{LR}}| = |\partial\Omega|$), independent of the state interior or lattice size. The Hamiltonians are further spectrally reshaped to open a protective gap around the target eigenstate. We validate our approach in discrete lattices and acoustic metamaterials. We provide a database of over 100 target states and a customizable design program, demonstrating the generality and extensibility of our method. Although demonstrated in acoustics, the principle can be applied to thermal, electrical-circuit and photonic metamaterials.

### Paper [357]
Title: Maximum-Entropy Ensemble of Quantum Subsystems with a Fixed Average State
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39712
Authors: Charlie Shakeshaft, Christopher David White, Harry J. D. Miller
Abstract: The reduced density matrices of Haar-random bipartite states form a fixed-trace version of the Wishart ensemble of random matrices, with an asymptotic eigenvalue density given by the Marchenko-Pastur (MP) law. We establish a similar universal form for the maximum-entropy distribution of mixed states subject to a fixed ensemble average, arguing that its eigenvalue density converges asymptotically to that of the correlated Wishart ensemble (CWE). The density is thus obtained by a free multiplicative convolution formula, which we apply to the transverse-field Ising model (TFIM) with a thermal average state to derive a high-temperature leading-order correction to the MP law. We then consider measurement by a random POVM on the purification of the mixed reference state. We show that the resulting mixed-state ensemble is asymptotically equivalent to the CWE, and provide confirming numerical evidence. We return to the TFIM example to compute the entanglement of the system with the ancilla after measurement, and give finite-temperature Page curves for the average entanglement entropies.

### Paper [358]
Title: Semiclassical Wave Packet Dynamics For Nonlinear Schr\"odinger Equations with Harmonic Potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01108
Authors: Jared Smith, Tomoki Ohsawa
Abstract: We propose an approximate Hamiltonian dynamics for the Gross--Pitaevskii equation $i\hbar\psi_t = -\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\Delta \psi + V(x)\psi + \hbar^2 \lambda |\psi|^2\psi$ with harmonic potential $V(x)$ and $x\in\mathbb{R}^d$. We do this by extending the Gaussian ansatz of Perez-Garcia et al.~(Phys.~Rev.~A 56, 1424 (1997)) to the semiclassical wave packets of Hagedorn. We derive the evolution equations for the wave packets from the Hamiltonian/symplectic perspective, rather than the Lagrangian/variational one taken by Perez-Garcia et al. We first show that these two perspectives yield identical evolution equations in the Gaussian case. We then derive the evolution equations for an arbitrary element of the semiclassical wave packets. Finally, we demonstrate the efficacy of the approximate dynamics with numerical experiments.

### Paper [359]
Title: The massive phase of the two-dimensional Heisenberg model is stable under small anisotropy
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01532
Authors: Yan Ru Pei
Abstract: Polyakov predicted that the classical Heisenberg model on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ has exponentially decaying correlations at every temperature, and proofs of this have recently been posted. We show that the massive phase survives small anisotropy. For every inverse temperature $\beta$ there is $\delta(\beta)>0$ such that the $S^2$-valued model with couplings $\sum_iJ^i\sigma_x^i\sigma_y^i$ has exponentially decaying spin two-point functions, uniformly in finite subgraphs with free boundary conditions and in edge strengths up to $\beta$, whenever all $|J^i-1|\le\delta(\beta)$. The same holds for aligned single-ion anisotropy and for nearly isotropic $O(n)$ models with $n\ge3$. Consequently, for the easy-plane model with couplings $\sigma^x\sigma^x+\sigma^y\sigma^y+\lambda\sigma^z\sigma^z$, the onset of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless phase, proved by Garban and de Montgolfier for every $\lambda\in(-1,1)$, moves to zero temperature as $\lambda\uparrow1$. These results rest on the annulus crossing bound of Ding, Huang and Yang, used on one finite annulus where the anisotropy is a bounded perturbation, and on a sign-cluster argument with Ginibre's inequality for anisotropic planar energies. Explicit constants of another isotropic proof give decay for $\beta\le c |\log(1-\lambda)|^{1/2}$. For $n\ge4$ and $|J^i-1|\le\delta$ with $\delta$ small, at $\beta\le(1-\varepsilon)|\log\delta|/(2\pi)$ spin one-point functions decay exponentially away from the boundary under every boundary condition, and two-point functions decay, both on the scale $e^{\pi\beta+O(\sqrt{\beta\log\beta})}$. This is the $O(4)$ threshold: for $n=4$ it matches the renormalization-group crossover to leading order; for $n\ge5$ it lies below the prediction. This part is conditional on estimates of a recent manuscript on the $O(4)$ model, which the proof transfers to anisotropic couplings at one scale; only odd observables transfer.

### Paper [360]
Title: Quantifying out-of-distribution data severity with Wasserstein distances: benchmarking large language models for alloy property prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03767
Authors: YuJie Liu, Dezhen Xue, Jun Sun, Xiangdong Ding, Zhibin Gao, Turab Lookman
Abstract: General-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rival domain-specific pipelines on scientific prediction, but their performance under distribution shift is untested. Across six alloy and crystal-structure datasets, we benchmark LLMs against traditional, BERT-based, TabPFN, and text-regression baselines under random cross-validation and six out-of-distribution (OOD) protocols shifting input and target distributions, with severity quantified via a standardized Wasserstein distance. We isolate the LLM's advantage over k-nearest-neighbor (k-NN) retrieval: it beats k-NN on all eight tasks at a fixed 5% reference budget, on seven of eight when independently optimized, and in 72.5% of 120 OOD task--protocol configurations against an optimized baseline. No model family is uniformly best; the LLM is often strongest under severe shift, and error increases with target-space but not representation-space severity. Self-reported LLM confidence tracks both shift and error, more consistently than a density-only control from the same retrieved samples, and a matched-cohort check finds no substantial evidence of memorization.

### Paper [361]
Title: Exact Transport-Information Correspondence for Distribution-Adaptive Discretization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06059
Authors: Koretaka Yuge
Abstract: Discretization of continuous probability distributions is an operation on the sample space, and the structure induced by this operation cannot generally be determined solely from information geometry. In our recent work on unavoidable canonical nonlinearity (UCN), we established a non-arbitrary connection between the transport cost associated with discretization and the information-geometric indistinguishability of continuous probability distributions. Through this transport-information correspondence, UCN quantifies the information-theoretic cost associated with discretization in terms of the expected Kullback-Leibler divergence between nearby continuous distributions characterized by the discretization geometry. In the original UCN construction, discretization is implemented by transporting the probability mass within each cell to its geometric centroid. The resulting transport-information correspondence, however, is derived under specific conditions involving the cell geometry, spatial coverage, and the vanishing-cell-scale limit. In this work, we generalize the local-map discretization framework by allowing an arbitrary representative point for each cell and establish an exact decomposition of the associated transport cost into contributions from the conditional covariance of the probability distribution and the displacement of the representative point from its probability-weighted centroid. Consequently, the resulting transport cost is exactly represented by the expectation of the associated Fisher-induced quadratic form, without requiring uniform cell geometry, complete coverage of the sample space, or an infinitesimal cell-scale limit. The proposed construction thus establishes an exact transport-information correspondence for finite-scale discretization using arbitrary collections of non-overlapping bounded sets.

### Paper [362]
Title: Quasi-Normal Modes and thermalization in integrable Quantum Field Theories from the Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06473
Authors: Zoltan Bajnok, Romuald A. Janik
Abstract: We propose a Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA) approach for describing thermalization in integrable quantum field theories. The exponential damping and oscillations when approaching thermal equilibrium can be understood as the analogs of quasi-normal mode (QNM) frequencies of black hole perturbations within the framework of holography. Holographic intuitions and some general properties of thermal Green's functions motivate the existence of such a TBA formulation, as well as lead to the imposition of a nonstandard analytical structure on the QNM TBA, while comparison with integrable form-factor expansions fixes the remaining ingredients. We conjecture the QNM TBA to be equivalent to performing an all-order resummation of secular terms in a thermal form factor expansion of retarded 2-point correlation functions. We compare the predictions with tensor network simulations of the E8 deformation of Ising CFT at nonzero temperature. We conjecture that the QNM TBA framework should also be applicable for describing out-of-equilibrium approach to Generalized Hydrodynamics (GHD).

### Paper [363]
Title: Accessing Continuous Monodromy Defects of the 3D Conformal Field Theory on the Fuzzy Sphere
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08102
Authors: Yin Tang, W. Zhu
Abstract: Monodromy defects are line defects at the boundary of a topological symmetry surface, around which charged operators acquire a symmetry phase. They probe how a critical system responds to an inserted symmetry flux, but their universal information at strongly coupled fixed points, in particular for a continuous family of twists, remains hard to access. Here we investigate the continuous family of $U(1)$ monodromy defect with fractional twist angles embedded in the three-dimensional $O(2)$ Wilson--Fisher conformal field theory. We realize this continuous family of defects within the fuzzy-sphere framework by threading a fractional Aharonov--Bohm flux through the two poles of the sphere. We observe charged defect operators carrying fractional transverse spins, as a characteristic signature of the imposed monodromy, and a displacement operator close to its protected dimension, supporting the conformal nature of the defect line. Crucially, we resolve no relevant symmetry-preserving scalar, and identify the charge-one defect operators as the only relevant, symmetry-breaking deformations. Assuming the particle--vortex duality, the U(1) monodromy defect maps to a Wilson line of fractional charge in the critical noncompact Abelian Higgs model, and our results then provide evidence that these Wilson lines with fractional charge are locally stable conformal lines.

### Paper [364]
Title: Quantitative Assessment of Crystal Structure Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10380
Authors: Sergio Rinc\'on, Gabriel Gonz\'alez, Nicol\'as Andrade, Rafael Vel\'asquez, Sebasti\'an Ojeda, Juanita Puentes, Nicol\'as Aparicio, Paula C\'ardenas, Pablo Arbel\'aez
Abstract: Crystal Structure Prediction (CSP) aims to predict a material's 3D structure from its chemical composition, with applications from energy storage to biomedical devices. Despite its importance, existing evaluation frameworks suffer from key limitations: they rely on threshold-sensitive or incomparable metrics, focus predominantly on geometric performance while overlooking physical plausibility, and fail to account for polymorphism, where a chemical composition can crystallize into multiple stable structures. In parallel, recent Symmetry-Informed CSP (SICSP) methods often report results alongside standard CSP baselines while receiving crystallographic templates at inference, which constrain the search space and make SICSP a fundamentally different task. To address these limitations, we introduce an Orientation-invariant, Polymorph-Aware, Large Benchmark (OPAL-Bench) that evaluates CSP and SICSP as separate tasks. We evaluate 7 CSP and 2 SICSP methods under OPAL-Bench across 4 datasets using threshold-free geometric and physical distance metrics. Our results show that no method dominates across all metrics, and that geometric performance is not strongly correlated with physical plausibility. We also release OPAL-CSP, an SE(3)-equivariant flow-matching baseline for the CSP track that performs competitively on geometric metrics and ranks among the best on physical ones. By unifying geometric and physical metrics under a polymorph-aware protocol, OPAL-Bench enables a more comprehensive assessment of generative quality in CSP.
